can detect this
via the size-2 history
\{a,c\}\subseteq T_{p_{1}}
.
But this cannot be said for the violating system

p_{11}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}a.\mathbf{0}\!+\!r.s.(a.\mathbf{0}\!+\!c.\mathbf{0})
.
Since
p_{11}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56458pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xRightarrow{\;c\;}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56458pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xRightarrow{\;c\;}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.40128pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xRightarrow{\;c\;}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.40128pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xRightarrow{\;c\;}}}}
,
monitor
m_{7}
cannot use the previous size-2 history
and instead requires the size-3 history,
\{a,rsa,rsc\}\subseteq T_{p_{11}}
.
Similarly, the violating system
p_{14}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}a.\mathbf{0}\!+\!r.s.(a.\mathbf{0}\!+\!r.s.(a.\mathbf{0}\!+\!c.\mathbf{0}))

can only be detected
via a history containing the traces
\{a,rsa,rsrsa,rsrsc\}
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Example

VII.3

illustrates how execution upper bounds cannot be easily determined
from the structure of a formula.
However, the calculation of execution lower bounds from the formula structure is attainable.
For instance, the lower bound for a conjunction
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
would be the least bound between the lower bounds of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
respectively.
Crucially, history
lower
bounds are invariant w.r.t. recursive formula unfolding.

Example VII.4
.

Recall
\varphi_{8}
from
Example

VII.3

with a history lower bound of size 2, which is equal to the number of disjunctions
in
\varphi_{8}
plus 1 (as argued in
Example

VII.1
).
By the semantics in
Figure

1
, the same
systems
also violate the unfolding of
\varphi_{8}
,

i.e.,

\displaystyle\varphi_{8}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\;[a]\mathsf{ff}\vee\bigl([c]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[r][s](\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.([a]\mathsf{ff}\vee([c]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[r][s]\mathsf{X})))\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=[a]\mathsf{ff}\vee([c]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[r][s]\varphi_{8})

since

\varphi_{8}\equiv\varphi^{\prime}_{8}
.
A naive analysis would conclude that
\varphi_{8}^{\prime}
contains 2 disjunctions,
thereby requiring histories of
size 3.
But a compositional approach based on

Example

VII.2

would
allow us to conclude that
lower bounds of size 2 suffice.
To reject a violating SUS for
\varphi_{8}^{\prime}
, trace evidence is needed to determine violations for
both
sub-formulae
[a]\mathsf{ff}
and
[c]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[r][s]\varphi_{8}
.
Whereas 1 trace suffices to reject
[a]\mathsf{ff}
,
determining the lower bounds for rejecting
[c]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[r][s]\varphi_{8}
amounts to calculating the
least
lower bound required to reject either
[c]\mathsf{ff}
or
[r][s]\varphi_{8}
.
Since rejecting
[c]\mathsf{ff}
requires only 1 trace,
the total lower bound is that of
1+1=2
traces,
which is equal to that
of
\varphi_{8}
.

\hfill\blacksquare

The function
\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(-)

formalises the calculation of history lower bounds based on the syntactical analysis of formulae.

Definition VII.1
.

\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(-):\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\mathsf{ff})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}0

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi)\qquad\mathop{\textsl{lb}}([\alpha]\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\mathsf{tt})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\infty

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textsl{min}\{\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi),\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\psi)\}

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\mathsf{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\infty

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi\vee\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi)+\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\psi)+1

\hfill\blacksquare

There is one further complication
when calculating the number of trace prefixes required from the syntactic structure of formulae.
Our implicit assumption has been that, for disjunctions
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
, the incorrect system behaviour described by
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
is distinct.
Whenever this is not the case, formulae do not observe the lower bound proposed above since
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
might be violated by
common trace prefixes.

Example VII.5
.

Although analysing
\varphi_{9}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}[r]\mathsf{ff}\vee[r][s]\mathsf{ff}
syntactically gives the lower bound 2,
m_{8}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}r.\mathsf{no}\!\oplus\!r.s.\mathsf{no}\!=\!\llparenthesis\varphi_{9}\rrparenthesis
rejects all violating
systems
with the single prefix
rs
.

\hfill\blacksquare

We limit our calculations to a subset of
recHML
ruling out overlapping violating behaviour across disjunctions.

\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}
(below) combines universal modalities and disjunctions into one construct,
\bigvee_{i\in I}[\alpha_{i}]\varphi_{i}
, to represent the formula
[\alpha_{1}]\varphi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee[\alpha_{n}]\varphi_{n}
for the finite set index
I{=}\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Definition VII.2
.

\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}\subseteq\textsc{recHML}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\varphi,\psi{\in}\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{::=}\!\mathsf{tt}\!\;\mid\;\!\mathsf{ff}\!\;\mid\;\!\varphi\wedge\psi\!\;\mid\;\!\bigvee\nolimits_{\!\!i\in I}[\alpha_{i}]\varphi_{i}\!\;\mid\;\!\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\varphi\!\;\mid\;\!\mathsf{X}

where
\forall i,j\in I
, we have
i\neq j
implies
\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{j}
.

\hfill\blacksquare

To faciliate the statement and establishment of results on history lowerbounds, we define an
explicit
witness-based violation relation
H{\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}}\varphi
that avoids the existential quantifications over SUS histories of
Definitions

IV.1
and
IV.2
.
The new judgement
H{\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}}\varphi
corresponds to
p{\notin}\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
whenever
H{\subseteq}T_{p}
.

Definition VII.3
.

Given a predicate on
tAct
denoted as
Det
, the
violation relation
, denoted as
\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
, is the least relation
of the form

(\textsc{Hst}\times\textsc{Bool}\times\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}})

satisfying the
rules

\frac{H\neq\emptyset}{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\mathsf{ff}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vF}}\quad\frac{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\varphi$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]}{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\varphi}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vMax}}\quad\frac{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi}{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\wedge\psi}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vAndL}}\frac{(H,\mathsf{true})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\quad(H,\mathsf{true})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\psi}{(H,\mathsf{true})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\vee\psi}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vOr}}\frac{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\psi}{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\wedge\psi}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vAndR}}

\frac{H^{\prime}{=}\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\gamma)\quad\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}{=}\textsf{f\,}{\wedge}\textsc{Det}(\alpha)\quad(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}[\alpha]\varphi}{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}[\alpha]\varphi}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vUmPre}}\frac{H^{\prime}{=}\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\alpha)\quad\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}{=}\textsf{f\,}{\wedge}\textsc{Det}(\alpha)\quad(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi}{(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}[\alpha]\varphi}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize vUm}}

We read “
H
violates
\varphi
”,
H{\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}}\varphi
, when
(H,\mathsf{true})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Theorem

VII.1
shows that whenever a system
p
produces a history
H
that violates a formula
\varphi
,
i.e.,

H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
, then

p
must also violate it,
i.e.,

p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
(for
arbitrary
ILTSs).
To show correspondence in the other direction,
Theorem

VII.2
, we need to limit ILTSs to deterministic internal actions.
The reason for this is, once again, the set of systems such as
p_{10}
from
Example

V.4
for which there is
no
history
H\subseteq T_{p_{10}}
such that
H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi_{2}
, even though
p_{10}\notin\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket
.

Theorem VII.1
.

For all
formulae
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
,
if
\bigl(\exists H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\bigr)
then
p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.
∎

Theorem VII.2
.

Suppose
\textsc{Det}(\gamma){=}\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma{\in}\textsc{iAct}
.
For all

\varphi{\in}\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
,
if
p{\notin}\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
then
\bigl(\exists H{\subseteq}T_{p}

s.t.

H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\bigr)
.
∎

The new judgment allows us to state and verify that disjunction sub-formulae must be violated by
disjoint

histories.

Proposition VII.3
.

For all

\varphi\vee\psi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}
,
if
H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi\vee\psi
then
H=H^{\prime}\!\uplus\!H^{\prime\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
and
H^{\prime\prime}\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\psi
.
∎

Theorem

VII.4
establishes a lower bound on the
trace prefixes required to detect violations for
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}
formulae.

Theorem VII.4

(Lower Bounds)
.

For all

\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}
and

H\in\textsc{Hst}
,
if
H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
then
|H|\geq\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi)+1
.
∎

Example VII.6
.

Following
Theorem

VII.4
,
\varphi_{2},\varphi_{4},\varphi_{8}\!\in\!\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}
cannot be violated with fewer than 2 trace prefixes since
\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi_{2})\!=\!\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi_{4})\!=\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi_{8})\!=\!1
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Theorem

VII.4

also provides
a simple syntactic check to determine whether

\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}}
formulae are worth
monitoring for, according to
Definition

V.1
.

Corollary

1

shows that whenever
\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi)\!=\!\infty
,
formula
\varphi
is always satisfied,
i.e.,
violations for it can
never
be detected, regardless of the system being runtime verified.

Corollary 1
.

\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\textsc{nf}})\!=\!\infty

implies

\forall H\cdot H\not{\models_{Det}}\varphi
.
∎

Finally, we note that
although a minimum of
n
trace prefixes might be required by
Definition

VII.1
for analysis, the SUS might need to be executed
more
than
n
times to obtain these prefixes.
Intuitively, this is caused by redundancies in the monitors and the manner in which said monitors record trace prefixes, as illustrated in
Example

VII.7
.

Example VII.7
.

Assuming
\textsc{Det}(a){=}\mathsf{true}
, consider
\varphi_{10}
, describing the property
“after any number of serviced queries interspersed by
sequences of memory allocations, a system that can allocate memory cannot also perform a close action.”

\displaystyle\varphi_{10}

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\;\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\bigl([r][s]\mathsf{X}\wedge[a]\mathsf{X}\wedge([a]\mathsf{ff}\vee[c]\mathsf{ff})\bigr)

When synthesising
\llparenthesis\varphi_{10}\rrparenthesis
, we get monitor
m_{2}
from
Example

III.2
.
The system
p_{13}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.r.s.\mathsf{X}\!+\!a.X\!+\!a.c.\mathbf{0}
violates
\varphi_{10}
,
and
m_{2}

can reject it via the history
H=\{rsaa,rsac\}\subseteq T_{p_{13}}
,
in line with
Theorem

VII.4

since
\mathop{\textsl{lb}}(\varphi_{10})\!+\!1=2
trace prefixes.
However, the incremental manner with which traces are aggregated (
Section

III
) requires that, whenever
rsaa\in H
,
then
rsa\in H
as well.
This is due to the fact that for the
trace
rsa\cdots
, we always have
\emptyset\triangleright(\epsilon,m_{2})\triangleleft\,p_{14}\xRightarrow{\;rsa\;}\emptyset\triangleright(rsa,\mathsf{no})\triangleleft\,p^{\prime}_{14}
during the first monitored execution.
Thus, although 2 prefixes are sufficient to detect a violation, the operational mechanism for aggregating the traces for analysis forces us to observe at least
3
SUS executions
to gather the necessary traces for analysis.

\hfill\blacksquare

VIII
Related Work

Various bodies of work employ monitors over multiple runs for RV purposes.
The most prominent
target
Hyperproperties
,
i.e.,
properties describing sets of traces called
hypertraces
,
used to describe safety and privacy requirements
[
53
]
.
Finkbeiner
et al.

[
54
]
investigate the monitorability of hyperproperties expressed in HyperLTL
[
55
]
and identify three classes for monitoring hypertraces:
the bounded sequential, the unbounded sequential and the parallel classes.
They also develop a monitoring
tool
[
56
]
that analyses
hypertraces sequentially by converting an alternation-free HyperLTL formula into an alternating automaton
that is executed over
permutations of the observed traces.
They show that deciding monitorability for alternation-free HyperLTL formulae in this class is PSPACE-complete but undecidable in general.
Our
setup fits their unbounded sequential class because monitors receive each trace in sequence, and a SUS may exhibit an unbounded number of traces.
Agrawal
et al.

[
57
]
give a semantic characterisation for monitorable HyperLTL hyperproperties called
k
-safety.
They also identify syntactic HyperLTL fragments and show they are
k
-safety properties, backed up by a monitor synthesis algorithm that generates a combination of petri-nets and
LTL_{3}
monitors
[
58
]
.
Stucki
et al.

[
59
]
show that many properties in HyperLTL
involving
quantifier alternation cannot be monitored for.
They also present a
methodology for properties with one alternation by combining static verification and RV: the static part extracts information about the set of traces that the SUS can produce (
i.e.,
branching information about the number of traces in the SUS, expressed as a symbolic execution tree) that is used
by monitors to convert quantifications into
k
-(trace)-quantifications.

Despite the similarities of using multi-run monitoring, these works differ from ours in a number of ways.
For instance, the methods used are very different.
Our monitor synthesis algorithm is directly based on the formula syntax and does not rely on auxiliary models such as alternating automata or petri-nets, which facilitates syntactic-based proofs.
The results presented are also substantially different.
Although
[
57
,
59
]
prove that their monitor synthesis algorithm is sound, neither work considers completeness results,
maximality
or execution lower bound estimation.
More importantly, our target logic,
recHML
, is intrinsically different from (linear-time) hyperlogics since it (and other branching-time logics) is interpreted over LTSs,
whereas hyperlogics are defined over sets of traces.
which inherently coarser than an LTSs.
For instance,
the
systems
a.b.\mathbf{0}+a.c.\mathbf{0}
and
a.(b.\mathbf{0}+c.\mathbf{0})
are described by different LTSs but have an identical trace-based model,
i.e.,

\{ab,ac\}
;
this was a major source of complication for our technical development.
Even
deterministic LTSs
where the system
a.b.\mathbf{0}+a.c.\mathbf{0}
is disallowed,
it remains unclear how the two types of logics correspond.
For one, hyperlogics employ existential and universal quantifications over traces, which are absent from our logic.
If we had to normalise these differences (
i.e.,
no trace quantifications), a reasonable mapping would be to take a a linear-time interpretation,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\textsc{LT}}

[
11
,
23
]
for every
recHML
branching-time property
\varphi
, and require it to hold for all of its traces:
For all

\varphi
and deterministic systems
p
, we would
then expect

p{\in}\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
iff

T_{p}{\in}\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\textsc{LT}}
.
But even this correspondence fails,

e.g.

[a]\mathsf{ff}\vee[b]\mathsf{ff}
, describes
systems
that cannot perform both
a
and
b
actions and
a.\mathbf{0}+b.\mathbf{0}
clearly violates it.
However, with a linear-time interpretation, this formula denotes a
tautology
: it is satisfied by
all
traces since they are necessarily either not prefixed with an
a
action or with a
b
action.
There are, however, notable similarities between our history evaluation (
Figure

3
) and team semantics for temporal logics
[
60
,
61
]
, and
this relationship is worth further investigation.

The closest work to ours is
[
25
]
, where Aceto
et al.
give a framework to extend the capabilities of monitors.
They study monitorability under a grey-box assumption where, at runtime, a monitor has access to additional SUS information, linked to the system’s states, in the form of decorated states.
The additional state information is parameterised by a class of
conditions
that represent different situations, such as access to information about that state gathered from previous system executions.
Other works have also examined how to use prior knowledge about the SUS to extend monitorability in the linear-time and branching-time settings,
e.g.

[
24
,
62
]
.
In contrast, we treat the SUS as a black-box.

Multiple traces are also used to runtime verify traces with imprecise event ordering
[
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
]
due to interleaved executions of components.
Parametric trace slicing
[
64
,
65
]
infers additional traces from a trace with interleaved events
by traversing the original trace and dispatching events to the corresponding slice.
Attard
et al.

[
66
]
partition the observed trace at the instrumentation level by synthesising monitors
attached to specific system components; they
hint at how this could enhance the monitoring expressive power for certain properties but do not prove any monitorability results.
Despite their relevance,
all traces
in
[
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
]

are
extracted from a
single
execution.

In
[
67
]
, Abramsky studies testing on multiple, yet finite, copies of the same system,
combining the information from multiple runs.
Our approach differs in three key aspects.
Firstly, our multiple executions correspond to creating multiple copies of the system from its initial state; Abramsky allows copies to be created at
any point
of the execution.
Secondly, tests are composed using parallel composition, can steer the execution of the SUS and can detect refusals.
In contrast, our monitors are composed using an instrumentation relation: they are passive and their verdicts are evidence-based (
i.e.,
what happened, not what could
not
have happened).
Third, the visibility afforded by monitor instrumentation considered in this work is larger than that obtained via parallel composition.

Silva
et al.

[
5
]
investigate combining traces produced by the same system
to create temporal models that approximates the SUS’s behaviour which can
then be used to
model check for branching-time properties.
This approach is
not
sound as the generated model may violate properties that are not violated by the actual system.
The authors advise using their
approach as a
complement to software testing
to suggest possible problems.

IX
Conclusion

We propose a framework to systematically extend
RV to
verify branching-time properties.
This is in sharp contrast to most research on RV, which centers around monitoring linear-time properties
[
21
,
22
]
.
As
shown in
[
11
]
, the class of monitorable linear-time (regular) properties is
syntactically larger than
that of monitorable branching-time properties,
explaining, in part, why the linear-time setting appears less restrictive when
runtime verified.
For instance, linear-time properties that are monitorable for violations are closed under disjunctions,
\varphi{\vee}\phi
, and existential modalities,
\langle\alpha\rangle\varphi
, as
these
can be
encoded in an effective, if not efficient, manner
[
11
,
68
]
, albeit in a setting with finite sets of actions.
In contrast, disjunctions and existential modalities in a branching-time setting cannot be encoded in terms of other
recHML
constructs.

We show that these limitations can be mitigated by observing multiple system executions.
Our results demonstrate that monitors can extract sufficient information over multiple runs to correctly detect the violation of a class of branching-time properties that may contain disjunctions (
Theorem

V.3
).
We also prove that the monitorable fragment
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
(
Definition

V.2
) is maximally expressive.
In particular, every property that can be monitored correctly using our monitoring framework can always be expressed as a formula in
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
.
Such a syntactic characterisation of monitorable properties is useful for tool construction.
It is worth pointing out that an implementation based on our theoretical framework could relax
assumptions
used only to attain completeness and maximality results;
e.g.
instead of assuming that all internal actions
are deterministic, a tool
could adopt a pragmatic stance and simply stop monitoring
as soon as a non-deterministic internal action is encountered, which would still yield a sound (but incomplete) monitor.
To validate the realisability of our multi-run monitoring RV framework, we outline a possible instantiation to actor-based systems.
We also show that the number of expected runs required to effect the runtime analysis can be calculated from the structure of the formula
being verified (as opposed to
other means
[
59
]
); see
Theorem

VII.4
.
We are unaware of similar results in the RV literature.

Future Work

We plan to investigate how our results can be extended
by considering more of a grey-box view of the system, in order to combine our machinery with techniques from existing work, such as
that of Aceto
et al.

[
25
]
.
We will also study strategies to optimise the collection of relevant SUS traces.
Depending on the application, one might seek to either maximize the information collected from every execution
(
e.g.
by continuing to monitor the same execution
after a trace prefix is added to the history)
or
minimize the runtime during which the monitor is active.
This investigation will be used for tool construction, possibly by extending existing (single-run) open-source monitoring tools for
recHML
such as
detectEr

[
12
,
44
]
that already target actor systems.
We also
plan to extend our techniques to other graph-based formalisms such as Attack/Fault Trees
[
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
]
used in cybersecurity, which often necessitate verification at runtime.

Appendix A
LTS Properties

We prove some general results about the LTS of
Section

II
and give the standard CCS notation (
Definition

A.1
), which is often used to describe systems.

Definition A.1
.

CCS processes
[
73
]
are inductively defined by the grammar
Prc
below:

p,q\in\textsc{Prc}\mathrel{::=}\mathbf{0}\;\;\;\mid\;\eta.p\;\;\;\mid\;p+q\;\;\;\mid\;\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.p\;\;\;\mid\;\mathsf{X}

The transition relation is defined as the least relation satisfying the rules

\frac{}{\eta.p\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}p}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize act}}\quad\frac{}{\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.p\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}p[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.p$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize rec}}\quad\frac{p\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}p^{\prime}}{p+q\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}p^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize selL}}\quad\frac{q\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}q^{\prime}}{p+q\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}q^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize selR}}

\hfill\blacksquare

The first result that we show is
Lemma

A.1
.

Lemma A.1
.

Whenever
p\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}p^{\prime}
and
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
where
t\in\textsc{tAct}^{*}
then either

•

\tau=\epsilon
and
p^{\prime}\equiv q^{\prime}
; or

•

there exist moves
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
and
p^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}q
.

Proof.

Follows from the confluence property of our ILTSes: silent (
\tau
)-transitions are confluent w.r.t. other actions (
Section

II
).
∎

Lemma A.2
.

If
p\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}q
then
T_{p}=T_{q}
.

Proof.

Let
p\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}q
.
We show that
T_{p}=T_{q}
in two parts.

•

Suppose
t\in T_{p}
, that is
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}
for some
p^{\prime}
.
We show
t\in T_{q}
, that is
q\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q^{\prime}
for some
q^{\prime}
.
This required matching move follows from
Lemma

A.1
.

•

Suppose
t\in T_{q}
. We show
t\in T_{p}
, that is
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}
for some
p^{\prime}
.
The required matching move is
p\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}q\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q^{\prime}
. ∎

Corollary 2
.

If
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
then
T_{p}=T_{q}
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

If
p\equiv q
then
T_{p}=T_{q}
.

Proof.

Suppose
p\equiv q
.
We show that
T_{p}=T_{q}
in two parts.

•

Suppose
t\in T_{p}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}
for some
p^{\prime}
.
By definition of
\equiv
, we know
\exists q^{\prime}
such that
q\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}\equiv q^{\prime}
, which means
t\in T_{q}
.

•

Suppose
t\in T_{q}
. The proof for showing
t\in T_{p}
is analogous. ∎

Lemma A.4
.

For all
\mu\in\textsc{tAct}
, if
p\xRightarrow{\;\mu\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}
and
p\xRightarrow{\;\mu\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
and
\textsc{Det}(\mu)=\mathsf{true}
then
T_{p^{\prime}}=T_{p^{\prime\prime}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
p\xRightarrow{\;\mu\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}
and
p\xRightarrow{\;\mu\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
By definition,
\exists q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}
such that

\displaystyle p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\mu\;}q_{2}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q_{3}\xrightarrow{\;\mu\;}q_{4}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}

We have to show that
T_{p^{\prime}}=T_{p^{\prime\prime}}
.
Repeatedly using the property of our ILTS that silent actions are confluent w.r.t. other actions (
Section

II
) and the assumption that
\textsc{Det}(\mu)=\mathsf{true}
, we obtain the dashed transitions in the diagram below.
p
q_{1}
q_{2}
p^{\prime}
q_{3}
q_{4}
p^{\prime\prime}
r_{1}
r_{2}
r_{3}
T
\mu
T
T
\mu
T
T
T
\mu
T
\mu
T
\equiv

By
Lemma

A.3
, we know
T_{r_{2}}=T_{r_{3}}
.
By
Corollary

2
, we also know
T_{p^{\prime}}=T_{q_{2}}=T_{r_{2}}
and
T_{p^{\prime\prime}}=T_{q_{4}}=T_{r_{3}}
.
We can thus conclude that
T_{p^{\prime}}=T_{p^{\prime\prime}}
.
∎

Proposition

A.5
shows the relation between the two forms of weak transitions, namely
\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}
and
\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}
.

Proposition A.5
.

For all systems
p,q{\in}\textsc{Prc}
, external actions
\alpha{\in}\textsc{eAct}
and internal actions
\gamma{\in}\textsc{iAct}
,

1.

if
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
then
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
;

2.

if
p\xRightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
then
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
;

3.

if
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
then
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
for some
t\in\textsc{iAct}^{*}
;

4.

if
p\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}q
then
p\xRightarrow{\;t\alpha t^{\prime}\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
for some
t,t^{\prime}\in\textsc{iAct}^{*}
;

5.

if
p\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
then
p\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}q
.

Proof.

We prove the above as follows:

To prove (1)

straightforward by definition.

To prove (2)

suppose
p\xRightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
.
By definition,
\exists p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}
such that
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}p^{\prime\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
.
By (1), we obtain
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}p^{\prime\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
.

To prove (3)

suppose
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
.
The proof proceeds by induction on the number of (strong) transitions
n
.
For the base case (
i.e.,

n=0
), then
p=q
and
p\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
.
For the inductive case (
i.e.,

n=k+1
),
then either
\exists p^{\prime}
such that
p\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
or

\exists p^{\prime},\gamma
such that
p\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
.
For the first subcase, by the IH, we obtain
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
for some
t\in\textsc{iAct}^{*}
, which implies
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
.
For the second subcase, by the IH, we obtain
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
, which implies
p\xRightarrow{\;\gamma t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
.

To prove (4)

suppose
p\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}q
.
By definition,
\exists p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}
such that
p\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}p^{\prime\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\;\;\;}q
.
By (3), we obtain
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}p^{\prime\prime}\xRightarrow{\;t^{\prime}\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
for some
t,t^{\prime}\in\textsc{iAct}^{*}
, which means
p\xRightarrow{\;t\alpha t^{\prime}\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}q
, as required.

To prove (5)

straightforward by definition and (1).
∎

We also prove
Proposition

II.1
(restated below), stating that equivalent systems satisfy the same formulae.

See
II.1

Proof.

Suppose
p\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and
p\equiv q
.
By definition,
p
and
q
are also strongly bisimilar
[
74
]
.
Our result,
q\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
, then follows by the well-known result that strong bisimulation preserves formula satisfactions.
∎

Appendix C
Monitor Correctness Proofs

In this section, we give the proofs for the instrumentation and monitor properties of
Section

IV
.

C-A

Instrumentation Properties

The proof of
Proposition

IV.1
relies on several technical lemmas that help us reason about the structure of the traces
t
in executing-monitors
(t,m)
.

Lemma C.1
.

(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
implies
t^{\prime}{=}t\alpha
.

Proof.

By rule induction.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

If
(t,m)\;\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}\;(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
then
t=t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By case analysis.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

If
(t,m)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
then
t=t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Follows from
Lemma

C.2
.
∎

See
IV.1

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
n
.

For the base case
, when
n=0
, the result is immediate.

For the inductive case
, when

n\!=\!k{+}1
, the transitions are as follows:

H\triangleright(\epsilon,m)\triangleleft\,p\xrightarrow{\;\eta_{1}\;}\cdots\xrightarrow{\;\eta_{k}\;}H^{\prime}\triangleright(t,m^{\prime})\triangleleft\,p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\eta_{k{+}1}\;}H^{\prime\prime}\triangleright(t^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\triangleleft\,p^{\prime\prime}

We show that
p\xRightarrow{\;t^{\prime}\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.

By the IH and
H\triangleright(\epsilon,m)\triangleleft\,p\xrightarrow{\;\eta_{1}\;}\cdots\xrightarrow{\;\eta_{k}\;}H^{\prime}\triangleright(t,m^{\prime})\triangleleft\,p^{\prime}
,
we obtain that

\displaystyle p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}

(2)

By case analysis,
H^{\prime}\triangleright(t,m^{\prime})\triangleleft\,p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\!\eta_{k{+}1}\!\;}H^{\prime\prime}\triangleright(t^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\triangleleft\,p^{\prime\prime}
could have been derived via
several rules:

•

Using rule
iNo
, then
p^{\prime\prime}=p^{\prime}
and
t=t^{\prime}
, which implies that
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
By (
2
), we conclude that
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Using rule
iTer
, then
t^{\prime}=t\alpha
and
p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}p^{\prime\prime}
for some
\alpha\in\textsc{eAct}
, which implies that
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
By (
2
), we conclude that
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Using rule
iAsS
, then
t=t^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}p^{\prime\prime}
, which implies that
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
By (
2
), we conclude that
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Using rule
iAsI
, then
t^{\prime}=t\gamma
and
p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}p^{\prime\prime}
for some
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
, which implies
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
By (
2
), we conclude that
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\gamma\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Using rule
iAsM
, then
p^{\prime}=p^{\prime\prime}
and
(t,m^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.
By
Lemma

C.2
, we obtain
t=t^{\prime}
, and since
p^{\prime}=p^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
Using (
2
), we conclude
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\epsilon\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Using rule
iMon
, then
p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}p^{\prime\prime}
and
(t,m^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
for some
\alpha\in\textsc{eAct}
.
By
Lemma

C.1
, we obtain that
t^{\prime}=t\alpha
, and since
p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}p^{\prime\prime}

we know that
p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
Using (
2
), we conclude
p\xRightarrow{\;t\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
i.e.,

p\xRightarrow{\;t\alpha\;}_{\!\raisebox{2.2604pt}{\tiny$T$}}p^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

C-B

Monitor Properties

In this section, we give the proof for
Propositions

IV.2
and
IV.3
from
Section

IV
.
However, we first give a few useful technical results about the executing-monitors of
Figure

1
.

Lemma C.4
.

For all
n\in\textsc{Mon}
,
if
(t,m)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,m^{\prime})
, then

(t,m\odot n)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,m^{\prime}\odot n)
.

Proof.

By induction on the number of
\tau
-transitions.
∎

Lemma

C.5
below asserts that a monitor that
\tau
-transition cannot transition along other actions.

Lemma C.5

(
\tau
-Race Absence)
.

If
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n)
then
(t,m)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 12.43855pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 12.43855pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.54193pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.54193pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}}}}
for all
\alpha{\in}\textsc{eAct}
.
∎

Proof.

Proof is straightforward by case analysis.
∎

Proposition

C.6
below assures us that monitor behaviour is confluent w.r.t.
\tau
-moves.
This allows us to equate monitor states up to
\tau
-transitions.

Proposition C.6

(
\tau
-confluence)
.

If

(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,m^{\prime})
and

(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,m^{\prime\prime})
,
there exist
moves
(t,m^{\prime})\,(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\,(t,n)
and
(t,m^{\prime\prime})\,(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\,(t,n)
for some
n\in\textsc{Mon}
.
∎

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,m^{\prime})
.

•

Case
mVrP1
. We have
(t,\mathsf{no}\odot n)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n)
where
t\in H
.
The second transition
(t,\mathsf{no}\odot n)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,m^{\prime\prime})
could have been derived in two ways:

–

Using rule
mVrP1
,
i.e.,

(t,\mathsf{no}\odot n)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n)
, which requires 0 matching moves.

–

Using rule
mTauR
,
i.e.,

(t,\mathsf{no}\odot n)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,\mathsf{no}\odot n^{\prime})
and
(t,n)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n^{\prime})
.
Since
t\in H
, we know
(t,\mathsf{no}\odot n^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n^{\prime})
by rule
mVrP1
.
This and
(t,n)\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n^{\prime})
give the required matching moves.

•

Case
mTauL
. We have
(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2})
because
(t,n_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime})
, which implies
n_{1}{\neq}\mathsf{no}
.
The transition
(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,m^{\prime\prime})
could have been derived using either of the following rules:

–

Rule
mVrP1R
,

i.e.,

(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1})
where
n_{2}=\mathsf{no}
and
t\in H
.
By
mVrP1R
, we deduce
(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime})
.
This and
(t,n_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime})
are the matching moves.

–

Rule
mVrP2R
,

i.e.,

(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,\mathsf{no})
where
n_{2}=\mathsf{no}
and
t\!\notin\!H
.
By rule
mVrPR2
, we deduce
(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,\mathsf{no})
.
This and
(t,\mathsf{no})(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{0}(t,\mathsf{no})
give the required matching moves.

–

Rule
mTauL
,

i.e.,
we have
(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime\prime}\odot n_{2})
and
(t,n_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime\prime})
.
By the IH, there exist moves
(t,n_{1}^{\prime})(\xrightarrow{\;\!\tau\!\;}_{H})^{*}(t,n)
and
(t,n_{1}^{\prime\prime})(\xrightarrow{\;\!\tau\!\;}_{H})^{*}(t,n)
for
n{\in}\textsc{Mon}
.
The matching moves,

(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2})(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}(t,n\odot n_{2})
and

(t,n_{1}^{\prime\prime}\odot n_{2})(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}(t,n\odot n_{2})
,
follow by
Lemma

C.4
.

–

Rule
mTauR
,

i.e.,
we have
(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2}^{\prime})
and
(t,n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime})
.
The required matching moves,

(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2}^{\prime})
and

(t,n_{1}\odot n_{2}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(t,n_{1}^{\prime}\odot n_{2}^{\prime})
,
follow by rules
mTauL
and
mTauR
.
∎

Corollary 3
.

If
(t,m)\,(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\,(t,m^{\prime})
and
(t,m)\,(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\,(t,m^{\prime\prime})
, then there must exist moves
(t,m^{\prime})\,(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\,(t,n)
and
(t,m^{\prime\prime})\,(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\,(t,n)
for some
n\in\textsc{Mon}
.

Proof.

Follows by repeatedly applying
Proposition

C.6
.
∎

Since, by
Proposition

C.6
, we can equate monitor states up
\tau
-transitions,
we define what it means for monitors to be equivalent up to
\tau
-moves,
Definition

C.1
below.

Definition C.1
.

Monitors
m
and
m^{\prime}
are
\tau
-equivalent, denoted as
m\approxeq_{H}m^{\prime}
, whenever for all
t\in\textsc{Trc}
, there exists
n\in\textsc{Mon}
such that

(t,m)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;})^{*}\;(t,n)
and
(t,m^{\prime})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;})^{*}\;(t,n)
.

Lemma C.7
.

\approxeq_{H}
is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

Proving
\approxeq_{H}
is symmetric and reflexive is straightforward.
To prove that
\approxeq_{H}
is transitive, suppose that
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{2})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{3})
.
By
Definition

C.1
, we know that there exist monitors
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
such that:

\displaystyle(t,m_{1})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n_{1})\text{ and }(t,m_{2})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n_{1})

\displaystyle(t,m_{2})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n_{2})\text{ and }(t,m_{3})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n_{2})

By
Corollary

3
, we know that there also exists some monitor
n
such that

(t,n_{1})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n)

and
(t,n_{2})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n)
,
which implies that

(t,m_{1})(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}(t,n)
and
(t,m_{3})(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}(t,n)
.
Our result, namely
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{3})
, follows by
Definition

C.1
.
∎

Lemma

C.8
below shows that two
\tau
-equivalent monitors must be equal if they can transition along the same external actions
\alpha\in\textsc{eAct}
.
Moreover, the executing-monitors reached after performing that transition are also equal.

Lemma C.8
.

If
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and
(t,n)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime})

where
(t,m)\approxeq_{H}(t,n)
,
then
m=n
and
m^{\prime}=n^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and
(t,n)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime})
where
(t,m)\approxeq_{H}(t,n)
.
By
Definition

C.1
, there exists some
n^{\prime\prime}
such that
(t,m)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n^{\prime\prime})
and
(t,n)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n^{\prime\prime})
.
But by

Lemma

C.5
, we also know
(t,m)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}
and
(t,n)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}
, which implies that
(t,m)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{0}\;(t,n^{\prime\prime})
and
(t,n)\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{0}\;(t,n^{\prime\prime})
, and thus
m=n^{\prime}=n
.

To show that if
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime})
then
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime\prime}
and
(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})\approxeq_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime})
, we use rule induction on
(t,m)\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
.
We outline the main cases:

•

Case
mEnd
. We have
(t,\mathsf{end})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t,\mathsf{end})
where
m=\mathsf{end}
.
Result follows immediately since the second transition
(t,\mathsf{end})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime})
could have only been derived using the rule
mEnd
, which implies
t^{\prime\prime}=t
and
m^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{end}
.

•

Case
mPar1
. We have
(t,m_{1}\odot m_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}\odot m_{2}^{\prime})
where
m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
because
(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime})
and
(t,m_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})
.
By
Lemma

C.5
, we know
(t,m_{1})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}
and
(t,m_{2})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.6831pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.96265pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}}}}
, which implies
m_{1}\neq\mathsf{no}
and
m_{2}\neq\mathsf{no}
.
This means that the second transition
(t,m_{1}\odot m_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime})
could have only been derived by
mPar1
.
Thus, we infer that
n^{\prime}\!=\!n_{1}\odot n_{2}
,
(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n_{1})
and
(t,m_{2})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},n_{2})
.
Our result,
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}
, follows by the IH.
∎

Similarly,
\tau
-equivalent monitors must be equal if they can (weakly) transition with the same trace
u\in\textsc{Trc}
, in which case the executing-monitors reached are also equal.

Lemma C.9
.

For all
u\!\in\!\textsc{Trc}
, if
(t,m_{1})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{1},n_{1})
and
(t,m_{2})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})
where

(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{2})
,
then
t_{1}\!=\!t_{2}
and
(t_{1},n_{1})\!\approxeq_{H}\!(t_{2},n_{2})
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on the length
l
of transitions in
(t,m_{1})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{1},n_{1})
.

•

For the
base case
, suppose
l=0
.
Then
u=\epsilon
,
m_{1}=n_{1}
and
(t,m_{2})(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}(t_{2},n_{2})
.
By
Lemma

C.3
, we know
t=t_{2}
.
By this and
Definition

C.1
, we also know
(t,m_{2})\approxeq_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})=(t,n_{2})
.
Since
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{2})
, our result,
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})
, follows via
Lemma

C.7
(transitivity).

•

For the
inductive case
, suppose
l=k\!+\!1
.
The transition sequence
(t,m_{1})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{1},n_{1})
can be expanded as

(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}_{H}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})\xRightarrow{\;u^{\prime}\;}_{H}(t_{1},n_{1})

where
u^{\prime},v_{1}\!\in\!\textsc{Trc}
,
n_{1}^{\prime}\!\in\!\textsc{Mon}
and
\eta\!\in\!\textsc{Act}\cup\{\tau\}
.
There are two subcases to consider:

–

When
\eta=\tau
, we have
(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})
and
u\!=\!u^{\prime}
, which implies
t\!=\!v_{1}
by
Lemma

C.3
and
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})
by
Definition

C.1
.
By
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{2})
and
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})
and
Lemma

C.7
, we obtain

(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{2})
.
By
(t,n_{1}^{\prime})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{1},n_{1})
, the original assumption
(t,m_{2})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})
and IH, we conclude
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
(t_{1},n_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})
.

–

When
\eta\!=\!\alpha{\in}\textsc{eAct}
, we have
(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})
and
u\!=\!\alpha u^{\prime}
for some
u^{\prime}\in\textsc{Trc}
.
The second sequence
(t,m_{2})\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})
can be expanded as

(t,m_{2})\;(\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}_{H})^{*}\;(t,n_{2}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(v_{2},n_{2}^{\prime\prime})\xRightarrow{\;u^{\prime}\;}_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})

where
v_{2}\in\textsc{Trc}
.
By
Definition

C.1
, we also know
(t,m_{2})\approxeq_{H}(t,n_{2}^{\prime})
.
From this, the original assumption that
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,m_{2})
and
Lemma

C.7
, we deduce
(t,m_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t,n_{2}^{\prime})
.
Since
(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})
and
(t,n_{2}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}_{H}(v_{2},n_{2}^{\prime\prime})
where
(t,m_{1}){\approxeq_{H}}(t,n_{2}^{\prime})
,
we obtain that
m_{1}=n_{2}^{\prime}
and
n_{1}^{\prime}=n_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
v_{1}=v_{2}
by
Lemma

C.8
.
Our result,
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
(t_{1},n_{1})\approxeq_{H}(t_{2},n_{2})
, follows by the IH.

–

When
\eta\!=\!\gamma{\in}\textsc{tAct}
, we must have
(t,m_{1})\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{H}(v_{1},n_{1}^{\prime})
and
u\!=\!\gamma u^{\prime}
for some
u^{\prime}\in\textsc{Trc}
.
However, this gives us a contradiction since by the rules in
Figure

2
,
(t,m_{1})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.93942pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.93942pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.12589pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{H}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.12589pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;\gamma\;}_{H}}}}
, meaning that this case never arises.
∎

We can now prove
Definition

E.3
from
Section

IV
, restated below.

See
IV.2

Proof.

Assume that
(t,m)\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and
(t,m)\xRightarrow{\;u\;}_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.
By
Lemma

C.7
, we know
(t,m)\approxeq_{H}(t,m)
.
By
Lemma

C.9
, we obtain that
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime\prime}
and
(t^{\prime},m^{\prime})\approxeq_{H}(t^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.
Our result then follows by
Definition

C.1
.
∎

We now show monitor rejections are irrevocable in terms of both additional traces,
width
, and longer traces,
length
.

See
IV.3

Proof.

The first part follows from
Lemma

C.10
below, letting
\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
.
The second part follows from
Lemma

C.11
below, letting
\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
.
∎

Lemma C.10
.

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},m)
implies
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},m)

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},m)
.

•

Case
no
. Follows immediately because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{no})
for all
H^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

•

Case
act
.
We know
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},\alpha.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},m)
where
H^{\prime}=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}((H,t),\alpha)
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}=\mathop{\textsl{od}}(\wedge)\textsc{Det}(\alpha)
.
There are two subcases to consider:

–

When
t=\alpha t^{\prime}
, then
H^{\prime}=(H^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}((H,t),\alpha)
for some
H^{\prime\prime}
.
By the IH, we deduce
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime}u),\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},m)
.
But by definition, we also know
(H^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime}u)=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}((H,tu),\alpha)
, meaning that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(\mathop{\textsl{sub}}((H,tu),\alpha),\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},m)
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},\alpha.m)
, follows by rule
act
.

–

When
t=\beta t^{\prime}
, we know by definition that
\mathop{\textsl{sub}}((H,t),\alpha)=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\alpha)=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}((H,tu),\alpha)
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},\alpha.m)
, follows immediately by applying rule
act
.

•

Case
actPre
. Proof is similar to that for
act
.

•

Case
parAL
.
We know that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},m^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime\prime})
because of
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},m^{\prime})
.
By the IH, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},m^{\prime})
.
Using rule
parAL
, we can conclude
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},m^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime\prime})
.

•

Case
parAR
. Proof is analogous to that for
parAR
.

•

Case
parO
.
We know
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\mathsf{true},m^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime\prime})
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\mathsf{true},m^{\prime})
and
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\mathsf{true},m^{\prime\prime})
.
By the IH,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\mathsf{true},m^{\prime})
and
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\mathsf{true},m^{\prime\prime})
.
Applying rule
parO
, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\mathsf{true},m^{\prime}{\otimes}m^{\prime\prime})
.

•

Case
rec
.
We know
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,t),\textsf{f\,},m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
.
By the IH, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}((H,tu),\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m)
, follows by rule
rec
.
∎

Lemma C.11
.

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m)
implies
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H\cup H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},m)

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m)
.
∎

Appendix D
Proving Monitorability

In this section, we prove
Theorem

V.3
from
Section

V
.
This theorem is proven in two steps; first, we show the monitors generated via the synthesis function
\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis

are sound,
Proposition

V.1
, and then we show they are complete,
Proposition

V.2
.
These rely on a number of results that use the alternative definition for property violations in
Definition

VII.3
as it is easier to establish results with it.
Concretely,
Lemmas

D.2
and
D.3
below show there is a tight correspondence between the rejected histories,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m)
, and violating histories,
(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
.

Lemma D.1
.

For all
\varphi,\psi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
,
\llparenthesis\varphi[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\psi$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\llparenthesis\psi\rrparenthesis$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

For all
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
,
if
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)
then
(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)
.
We outline the main cases:

•

Case
act
.
We know
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\alpha.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},m)
where

H^{\prime}=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\alpha)
and

\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}=\textsf{f\,}\wedge\textsc{Det}(\alpha)
and

\varphi=[\alpha]\psi
and
m=\llparenthesis\psi\rrparenthesis
.
By the IH, we obtain
(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\psi
.
Our result,
(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}[\alpha]\psi
, follows by rule
vUm
.

•

Case
rec
.
We know that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
where
\varphi=\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\psi
and
m=\llparenthesis\psi\rrparenthesis
.
By
Lemma

D.1
, we also know that

m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]=\llparenthesis\psi\rrparenthesis[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\llparenthesis\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\psi\rrparenthesis$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]=\llparenthesis\psi[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\psi$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\rrparenthesis
.
Using the IH, we then obtain
(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\psi[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\psi$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]
.
Our result,
(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\psi
, follows by rule
vMax
.
∎

See
V.1

Proof.

Expanding
Definition

IV.1
, we need to show that
for all
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
and
p\in\textsc{Prc}
,

if
\bigl(\exists H\subseteq T_{p}\text{ such that }\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)\bigr)

then
p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.

Suppose
\exists H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)
.
By
Lemma

D.2
, letting
\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
, we get
H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
.
Our result,
p\!\notin\!\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
, follows by
Theorem

VII.1
.
∎

Lemma D.3
.

For all
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
,
if
(H,\textsf{f\,})\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
then
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)
.

Proof.

Follows with a proof similar to that for
Lemma

D.2
.
∎

See
V.2

Proof.

Suppose that
\textsc{Det}(\gamma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
.
Expanding
Definition

IV.2
, we need to show that for all
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
and
p\in\textsc{Prc}
, we have that

if
p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket

then
\bigl(\exists H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)\bigr)

Suppose
p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.
By

Theorem

VII.1
, we know
\exists H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
H\models_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis)
, follow by
Lemma

D.3
, letting
\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
.
∎

We can now show
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}
is monitorable,
Theorem

V.3
.

See
V.3

Proof.

Follows from
Propositions

V.1
and
V.2
, with
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis
as the witness correct monitor.
∎

Appendix E
Proving Maximal Expressiveness

The first step towards showing that
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
is maximally expressive, namely
Theorem

V.4
,
is to define expressive-completeness w.r.t. the monitoring setup
Mon
of
Section

III
.

Definition E.1

(Expressive-complete)
.

A subset
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\textsc{recHML}
is
expressive-complete
if for all monitors
m\in\textsc{Mon}
,
there exists

\varphi\in\mathcal{L}
such that
m
monitors correctly for it.
∎

We prove that the language
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
is expressive-complete systematically, by concretising the existential quantification of a formula
\varphi
in
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
for every monitor
m
in
Mon
such that
m
monitors correctly for it (
Definition

IV.3
).

Definition

E.2
below formalises a function
\langle\!\langle-\rangle\!\rangle
that maps every monitor in
Mon
to a corresponding formula.

Definition E.2
.

The function
\langle\!\langle-\rangle\!\rangle:\textsc{Mon}\rightarrow\textsc{recHML}
is defined inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathsf{no}\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{ff}

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle m\oplus n\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\vee\langle\!\langle n\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\alpha.m\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}[\alpha]\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathsf{end}\,\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{tt}

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle m\otimes n\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\langle n\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathsf{X}\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{X}

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m\rangle\!\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle

∎

Note that,
\textbf{cod}(\langle\!\langle-\rangle\!\rangle)=\textsc{recHML}
as, when given arbitrary monitors, we have
no
guarantee that
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle=\varphi
is in
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
.

Example E.1
.

Recall monitor
m_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.(r.s.\mathsf{X}\otimes(a.\mathsf{no}\oplus c.\mathsf{no}))
from
Example

III.1
.
When
\textsc{Det}(r)=\mathsf{false}
, the formula
\langle\!\langle m_{1}\rangle\!\rangle=\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.[r][s]\mathsf{X}\wedge([a]\mathsf{ff}\vee[c]\mathsf{ff})=\varphi_{4}
is neither monitorable, according to
Definition

V.1
, nor does it belong to
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
, as shown in
Example

V.2
.
This occurs because parallel disjunction monitors prefixed with non-deterministic actions will generate formulas containing disjunctions prefixed with non-deterministic universal modalities.
∎

Definition

E.3
characterises a subset of monitors from
Mon
, parametrised by
eAct
and the associated action determinacy delineation defined by
Det
.
Similar to
Definition

V.2
, it employs a flag to calculate deterministic prefixes via rule
cAct
along the lines of
Figure

3
.
This is then used by rule
cOr
, which is only defined when the flag is
\mathsf{true}
.

Definition E.3
.

The judgement
\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m
for monitors
m\in\textsc{Mon}
and flag
\textsf{f\,}\in\textsc{Bool}
is defined coinductively as the
largest
relation satisfied by the following rules.

\frac{m\in\{\mathsf{end},\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{X}\}}{\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize cM}}\frac{\textsf{f\,}\wedge\textsc{Det}(\alpha)\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m}{\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\alpha.m}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize cAct}}\frac{\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m\;\;\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}n}{\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m\otimes n}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize cParA}}\frac{\mathsf{true}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m\;\;\mathsf{true}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}n}{\mathsf{true}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m\oplus n}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize cParO}}\frac{\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\varphi[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]}{\textsf{f\,}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize cRec}}

The set
\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{\,m\,\mid\,\mathsf{true}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}m\,\}
defines the set of monitors where all parallel disjunctions are prefixed by deterministic external actions (up to recursion unfolding).

\hfill\blacksquare

We can show that whenever we limit systems to deterministic internal actions (see
Example

V.4
), monitor
m\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
monitors correctly for the formula
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle
.
This relies on
Proposition

E.1
, asserting that
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
whenever
m\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
.

Proposition E.1
.

If
m\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
then
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
.
∎

Proposition E.2
.

Suppose
\textsc{Det}(\gamma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
.
For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, monitor
m
monitors correctly for
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle
.

Proof.

Pick
m\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
.
By
Proposition

E.1
, we know
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
.
We show that
m
is sound and complete for
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle
:

To prove soundness,

suppose
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
.
Since
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
, we can use
Proposition

V.1
, letting
\varphi=\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle
, to obtain that

p\notin\llbracket\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\rrbracket
.

To show completeness,

suppose
p\notin\llbracket\langle\!\langle\varphi\rangle\!\rangle\rrbracket
.
Since
\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
and
\textsc{Det}(\gamma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
, we can use
Proposition

V.1
, letting
\varphi=\langle\!\langle m\rangle\!\rangle
, to obtain that there exists
H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
.
∎

While we have demonstrated that a formula
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
exists for every monitor
m\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, we want to establish a stronger result: that a formula
\varphi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
exists for every monitor
m\in\textsc{Mon}
.
We show this by generating a monitor
m
in
\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
for each monitor
n
in
Mon
such that
m
and
n
reject the same histories.
This is done using the function
\mathcal{T}(-)
, formalised in
Definition

E.4
below.

The function
\mathcal{T}(-)
employs a flag to compute deterministic prefixes via rule
tAct
, which is then used by rule
tParF
to transform parallel disjunction monitors to the inactive monitor
\mathsf{end}
when the flag is
\mathsf{false}
.
Additionally, this function relies on a mapping
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}:\textsc{TVars}\rightarrow\textsc{Mon}\times\textsc{Bool}
.
When the transformation encounters a recursion monitor
\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m
with the flag
f
, the entry
\mathsf{X}\rightarrow\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle
is added to
\sigma
.
Recursion variables are unfolded if there is an entry for them in
\sigma
and have not already been visited with the current flag (rule
tTVar3
).

Definition E.4
.

Given a predicate on
tAct
denoted as
Det
, the function

\mathcal{T}:\textsc{Mon}\times\textsc{Bool}\times\textsc{Sub}\rightarrow\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}

is the smallest relation satisfied by the following rules.

\frac{}{\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{no},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{no}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tNo}}\frac{}{\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{end},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{end}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tEnd}}\frac{\mathsf{X}\notin\textbf{dom}(\sigma)}{\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{X},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{X}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tTVar1}}\frac{\sigma(\mathsf{X})=\langle m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle}{\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{X},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{X}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tTVar2}}\quad\frac{\sigma(\mathsf{X})=\langle m,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle\quad\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\neq\textsf{f\,}\quad\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=n}{\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{X},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=n}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tTVar3}}\frac{\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle])=n}{\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tRec}}\frac{\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,}\wedge\textsc{Det}(\alpha),\sigma)=n}{\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\alpha.n}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tAct}}\frac{\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=m^{\prime}\quad\mathcal{T}(n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=n^{\prime}}{\mathcal{T}(m\otimes n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=m^{\prime}\otimes n^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tParA}}\frac{\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma)=m^{\prime}\quad\mathcal{T}(n,\mathsf{true},\sigma)=n^{\prime}}{\mathcal{T}(m\otimes n,\mathsf{true},\sigma)=m^{\prime}\oplus n^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tParOT}}\quad\frac{}{\mathcal{T}(m\otimes n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)=\mathsf{end}}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize tParOF}}

We write
\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=n
whenever there exists a proof derivation satisfying that judgement.
As a shorthand, we also write
\mathcal{T}(m)
in lieu of
\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Proposition

E.3
establishes a correspondence between the monitors
m
and
\mathcal{T}(m)
.
Specifically, these monitors reject the same histories.

Proposition E.3
.

For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
and
H\in\textsc{Hst}
,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
iff
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathcal{T}(m))

Proof.

Since the proof is quite involved and relies on several additional results, we prove it in a separate subsection at the end of this section.
∎

Example E.2
.

Monitor
m_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.r.s.\mathsf{X}\otimes(a.\mathsf{no}\oplus c.\mathsf{no})

rejects a history
H
with flag
\mathsf{true}
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
, if and only if its unfolding does,

i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(r.s.m_{1}\otimes(a.\mathsf{no}\oplus c.\mathsf{no}),H,\mathsf{true})
.
When
\textsc{Det}(r)=\mathsf{false}
, we can show that
\neg\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,r.s.m_{1})
, which implies

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,a.\mathsf{no}\oplus c.\mathsf{no})
.
This means that
m_{1}
rejects all histories containing the traces
a
and
c
, corresponding to the histories rejected by the generated monitor
m_{6}
below.

\displaystyle m_{6}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.(r.s.(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.r.s.\mathsf{X}\otimes\mathsf{end})\otimes(a.\mathsf{no}\oplus c.\mathsf{no}))=\mathcal{T}(m_{1})

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\max{\mathsf{X}}{([r][s](\mathsf{max}\,\mathsf{\mathsf{X}}.[r][s]\mathsf{X}\wedge\mathsf{tt})\wedge([a]\mathsf{no}\vee[c]\mathsf{no}))}

Importantly, monitor
m_{6}
monitors correctly for the
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
formula
\varphi_{3}^{\prime}
above.

\hfill\blacksquare

Corollary 4
.

Suppose
\textsc{Det}(\gamma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
.
For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
, monitor
m
monitors correctly for
\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle
.

Proof.

Assume
\textsc{Det}(\gamma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
.
Pick
p\in\textsc{Prc}
.

To show soundness,

assume there exists
H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
.
By
Proposition

E.3
, we know
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathcal{T}(m))
.
Since
\textbf{cod}(\mathcal{T}(-))=\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, then
\mathcal{T}(m)\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
.
Thus, using
Proposition

E.2
, we conclude
p\notin\llbracket\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle\rrbracket
.

To show completeness,

assume that
p\notin\llbracket\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle\rrbracket
.
Since
\textbf{cod}(\mathcal{T}(-))=\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, we know
\mathcal{T}(m)\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, which by
Proposition

E.1
, implies that

\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
.
Thus, using
Proposition

E.2
, we deduce that there exists
H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathcal{T}(m))
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
, follows by
Proposition

E.3
.
∎

Equipped with these results, we can now prove
Theorem

E.4

Theorem E.4
.

If
\textsc{Det}(\alpha)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\alpha\in\textsc{eAct}
,

\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
is Expressive-Complete w.r.t.
Mon
.

Proof.

Pick
m\in\textsc{Mon}
.
By
Corollary

4
, we know monitor
m
monitors correctly for the formula
\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle
.
Also, since
\textbf{cod}(\mathcal{T}(-))=\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, we know
\mathcal{T}(m)\in\textsc{Mon}_{\textsc{Det}}
, which by
Proposition

E.1
, implies that

\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
, as required.
∎

We can now show that
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
is the largest monitorable subset of
recHML
up to logical equivalence,
Theorem

V.4
, restated below.

See
V.4

Proof.

Assume
\textsc{Det}(\gamma){=}\mathsf{true}
for all
\gamma\in\textsc{iAct}
.
Assume also that
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\textsc{recHML}
is monitorable w.r.t.
Mon
.
By
Definition

V.1
, this means that for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}
,

\exists m\in\textsc{Mon}\text{ such that }m\text{ monitors correctly for }\varphi

Pick
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}
and assume
\exists m\in\textsc{Mon}
such that
m
monitors correctly for it.
By
Definition

IV.3
, this means that for all
p\in\textsc{Prc}
,

\displaystyle p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket\text{ iff }\bigl(\exists H\subseteq T_{p}\text{ such that }\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)\bigr)

(3)

We need to show that
\exists\psi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
such that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
.

Using
Theorem

E.4
, for the monitor
m
used in (
3
), we also know

\exists\psi\in\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
where
\psi=\langle\!\langle\mathcal{T}(m)\rangle\!\rangle
and
m
monitors correctly for
\psi
.
Expanding
Definition

V.1
, this means that for all
p\in\textsc{Prc}
,

\displaystyle p\notin\llbracket\psi\rrbracket\text{ iff }\bigl(\exists H\subseteq T_{p}\text{ such that }\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)\bigr)

(4)

We prove
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
in two steps; first, we show
\llbracket\psi\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and then we show that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
.
For the former, assume an arbitrary
p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.
By (
3
), we know
\exists H\subseteq T_{p}
such that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
, which by (
4
) implies that
p\notin\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
.
We thus have

\displaystyle p\notin\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket\text{ implies }p\notin\llbracket\psi\rrbracket

(5)

By the contrapositive of (
5
),
we deduce that
p\in\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
implies
p\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
,
i.e.,

\llbracket\psi\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.
Dually, we can show
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
.
Our result,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
, follows.
∎

E-A

Proving
Proposition

E.3

The proof for
Proposition

E.3
relies on several additional results.

Lemma E.5
.

Suppose
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
.
Then for all
m,n\in\textsc{Mon}
,
\textsf{f\,},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\in\textsc{Bool}
and
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}
, we have

\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle])

Proof.

Suppose
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
.
The proof proceeds by induction on

m
.
The only interesting case is when
m=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle\quad=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle])

\displaystyle\quad=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle][\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle])

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ for some }n\text{ and }\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ since }\sigma[\langle n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle][\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle])

The other cases are straightforward.
∎

Lemma E.6
.

For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
,
\textsf{f\,}\in\textsc{Bool}
and
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}
,
if
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
and
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
then
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma))
.

Proof.

Follows from the contrapositive of
Lemma

E.7
.
∎

Lemma E.7
.

For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
,
\textsf{f\,}\in\textsc{Bool}
and
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}
,
if
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma))
then either
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
or
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma))
.
The proof proceeds by induction on the derivation of
\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)
.
We only outline the main cases:

•

Case
tVar3
,
i.e.,

\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=n
because
\sigma(\mathsf{Y})=\langle m,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle
where
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\neq\textsf{f\,}
, and
\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=n
.
Since
\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\textsf{f\,},\sigma))=\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)=\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma))
, we can use the IH and obtain that either
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
or
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
.
Since
\sigma(\mathsf{Y})=\langle m,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle
, then it must be that
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
.

•
