Case
tAct
,
i.e.,

\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\alpha.n
because
\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,}\wedge\textsc{Det}(\alpha),\sigma)=n
.
Since
\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma))=\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\alpha.n)=\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)=\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,}\wedge\textsc{Det}(\alpha),\sigma))
, we can use the IH and obtain that either
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
or
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
.
In turn, this implies that either
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\alpha.m)
or
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
.

•

Case
tRec
,

i.e.,

\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.n
because
\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})=n
where
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{Y}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]
.
Working up to
\alpha
-equivalence, we can assume that
\mathsf{X}\neq\mathsf{Y}
.
Since
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.n)=\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)\setminus\{\mathsf{Y}\}
, then
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)
.
By the IH, we obtain that either
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)
or
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma^{\prime}))
.
In case of the former, since
\mathsf{X}\neq\mathsf{Y}
, we deduce that
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m)
.
In case of the latter, there are two subcases.
If
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
, then we are done.
Otherwise, if
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma^{\prime}))
but
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
, then it must be that
\mathsf{X}\in\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m)
.
∎

Lemma E.8
.

Given
n\in\textsc{Mon}
and
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}
, suppose that
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
and
\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)\subseteq\{\mathsf{X}\}
.
Then for all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])=

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on the structure of
m
.

•

Case
m=\mathsf{X}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(n,\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(n,\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

since
\mathsf{X}
is not free

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

•

Case
m=\mathsf{Y}
.
There are two subcases to consider.
When
\mathsf{Y}\notin\textbf{dom}(\sigma)
, the proof is straightforward.
When
\sigma(\mathsf{Y})=\langle m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}\rangle
for some
m^{\prime}
and
f
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:subs-shadowing}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ because since }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))\text{ then }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ since }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:trnsf-preserves-fv}}

•

Case
m=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:subs-shadowing} since }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

since
\mathsf{X}
is not free

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

•

Case
m=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)

by the IH

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle])[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

The remaining cases are more straightforward.
∎

Lemma E.9
.

Given
n\in\textsc{Mon}
and
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}
, suppose
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
and
\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)\subseteq\{\mathsf{X}\}
.
For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
and
\textsf{f\,}\in\textsc{Bool}
,

\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

where
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]
.
Suppose
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
and
\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)\subseteq\{\mathsf{X}\}
.
Then
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n)
either.
The proof proceeds by induction on the structure of
m
.
We outline the main cases.

•

Case
m=\mathsf{X}
. There are three subcases to consider.

–

When
\sigma(\mathsf{X})=\langle m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}\rangle
for some
m^{\prime}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{X}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{X}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{X},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trnsfM}}

–

When
\sigma(\mathsf{X})=\langle m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle
for some
m^{\prime}
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\neq\textsf{f\,}
,
the proof is similar.

–

When
\mathsf{X}\notin\textbf{dom}(\sigma)
, the proof is similar.

•

Case
m=\mathsf{Y}
.
There are three subcases to consider.

–

When
\sigma(\mathsf{Y})=\langle m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}\rangle
for some
m^{\prime}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathsf{Y}\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trnsfM}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Y}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trnsfM}}

–

When
\sigma(\mathsf{Y})=\langle m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle
for some
m^{\prime}
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\neq\textsf{f\,}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trnsfM}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:subs-shadowing}}\text{ because since }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))

\displaystyle\quad\text{ then }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ since }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:trnsf-preserves-fv}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{Y},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trnsfM}}

–

When
\mathsf{Y}\notin\textbf{dom}(\sigma)
, the proof is straightforward.

•

Case
m=\alpha.n
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}((\alpha.m^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\alpha.(m^{\prime}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]),\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma)\text{ where }\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}=\textsf{f\,}\wedge\textsc{Det}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)\text{ using the IH }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}\rangle]

There are two subcases to consider.
If
\textsf{f\,}=\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\alpha.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]

\displaystyle=\bigl(\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\text{ as required}

Otherwise, if
\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}=\mathsf{false}
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\alpha.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false}\rangle]

\displaystyle=\alpha.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

since
\mathsf{X}
is not free

\displaystyle=\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\mathsf{true},\sigma^{\prime\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle\quad\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:change-flag} where }\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true}\rangle]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m^{\prime},\mathsf{true},\sigma^{\prime\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\mathsf{true},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\text{ as required}

•

Case
m=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}((\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma)\text{ since }\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]\text{ using \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:subs-shadowing} }

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime}[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}\rangle])

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime}[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}\rangle])\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

since
\mathsf{X}
is not free

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

•

Case
m=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}((\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\bigl(m^{\prime}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr),\textsf{f\,},\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ where }\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{Y}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime}[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,}\rangle]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\bigl(\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\sigma^{\prime},\textsf{f\,})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ by the IH where }\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}[X\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\textsf{f\,}\rangle]

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.\mathcal{T}(m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime\prime})\bigr)[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\sigma^{\prime},\textsf{f\,})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\sigma^{\prime},\textsf{f\,})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{Y}.m^{\prime},\textsf{f\,},\sigma^{\prime\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n,\sigma^{\prime\prime},\textsf{f\,})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:subs-shadowing} }

\displaystyle\qquad\text{since }\mathsf{Y}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\qquad\text{and the assumption }\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(n)\subseteq\{\mathsf{X}\}\text{ implies }\mathsf{Y}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n)

The remaining cases are more straightforward.
∎

Lemma

E.11
shows that the transformation function
\mathcal{T}(-)
preserves history rejections.
However, its proof relies on
Lemma

E.10
below.

Lemma E.10
.

Suppose that for all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
and
\sigma\in\textsc{Sub}
,
\mathsf{X}\notin\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(\textbf{cod}(\sigma))
and
\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(m)\subseteq\{\mathsf{X}\}
.

If
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma))

then
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma))
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by rule induction on the judgement
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma))
.
We outline the main cases.

•

Case
act
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\mathsf{false},\sigma))
where
\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\mathsf{false},\sigma)=\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma))
where
H^{\prime}=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\alpha)
.
There are two subcases:

If
\textsc{Det}(\alpha)=\mathsf{false}
,
\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\mathsf{false},\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\mathsf{true},\sigma)
,
which implies
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\mathsf{true},\sigma))
.

If
\textsc{Det}(\alpha){=}\mathsf{true}
, by the IH,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma))
.
Applying rule
act
, we get
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma))
.
Our result follows by the fact that
\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma)=\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\mathsf{true},\sigma)
.

•

Case
rec
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\mathsf{false},\sigma))

where, by
Definition

E.4
, we have

\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\mathsf{false},\sigma)=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma^{\prime})
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\mathsf{false}\rangle]

because

\displaystyle\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])

(6)

By
Lemma

E.9
, we also know that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma^{\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{false},\sigma)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\mathsf{false},\sigma)

(7)

By (
6
), (
7
) and the IH, we deduce
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\mathsf{true},\sigma))
,
which implies that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma^{\prime\prime})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma^{\prime\prime})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])

where
\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma[\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\mathsf{true}\rangle]
by
Lemma

E.9
.
Applying rule
rec
, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma^{\prime\prime}))
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\mathsf{false},\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\mathsf{true},\sigma))
, follows by
Definition

E.4
since
\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\mathsf{true},\sigma^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\mathsf{true},\sigma)
.
∎

Lemma E.11
.

For all
m\in\textsc{Mon}
,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m)
iff
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.

Proof.

For the “only if” direction,
the proof proceeds by rule induction on
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m)
. We outline the main cases.

•

Case
act
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\alpha.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},m)
where
H^{\prime}=\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\alpha)
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}=\textsf{f\,}{\wedge}\textsc{Det}(\alpha)
.
By the IH, we obtain that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\emptyset))
.
Applying rule
act
, we get
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\alpha.\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\emptyset))

which, by
Definition

E.4
, implies that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.

•

Case
actI
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\alpha.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\alpha.m)
where
H^{\prime}{=}\mathop{\textsl{sub}}(H,\gamma)
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}{=}\textsf{f\,}{\wedge}\textsc{Det}(\gamma)
for some
\gamma{\in}\textsc{iAct}
.
By the IH, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\emptyset))
.
There are two subcases to consider.
If
\textsf{f\,}=\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}
, we can apply rule
actI
and conclude
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.
If
\textsf{f\,}\neq\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}
,
i.e.,

\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
and
\textsf{f\,}^{\prime}=\mathsf{false}
, then by
Lemma

E.10
, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H^{\prime},\textsf{f\,}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.
Applying rule
actI
, we conclude that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(\alpha.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.

•

Case
rec
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m)
because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
.
By the IH, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.
Using
Lemma

E.9
and
Definition

E.4
, we know

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\emptyset)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

Let
n=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})
.
We thus have
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},n[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
.

By rule
rec
, we obtain
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n)
.
Our result,
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
, follows by
Definition

E.4
.

The proof for the “if” direction follows similarly by rule induction on
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.
The only case that differs slightly is that for
rec
.

•

Case
rec
.
We know
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
,
i.e.,

\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n)

where

\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n=\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset)=\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})

because
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},n[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
.
Using
Definition

E.4
and
Lemma

E.9
, we know

\displaystyle n[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m,\textsf{f\,},\{\mathsf{X}\mapsto\langle\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,}\rangle\})[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m,\textsf{f\,},\emptyset)$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\emptyset)

We can thus rewrite
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},n[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.n$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
as the judgement
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathcal{T}(m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}],\textsf{f\,},\emptyset))
.
By the IH, we obtain that
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},m[\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\mathsf{X}$}])
.
Applying rule
rec
, we conclude
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,\textsf{f\,},\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.m)
as required.
∎

We are now in a position to prove
Proposition

E.3
, restated below.

See
E.3

Proof.

Follows from
Lemma

E.11
, letting
\textsf{f\,}=\mathsf{true}
.
∎

Appendix F
Implementability Aspects

The verification technique presented in this paper lends itself well to the implementation of a tool that runtime verifies systems over multiple runs.
We outline the steps for a full automation and give a complexity analysis of this technique.

Algorithm.

The first step is to generate executable monitors from properties expressed as
\textsc{sHML}^{\vee}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}
formulae, following the synthesis algorithm of
Definition

V.3
.
These monitors must then be instrumented to execute alongside the SUS w.r.t. the history of traces observed thus far (initialised to empty) as
outline
monitors
[
21
]
, which allows us to treat systems as black-boxes.
Instrumentation forwards the events generated by the SUS to the monitor, which aggregates traces according to the mechanism in
Figure

1
.
Prior work
[
44
,
12
]
has shown that the synthesis and implementation of similar operational models is almost one-to-one.
Aceto
et al.

[
75
]
rigorously demonstrate their efficiency, which results in a stable tool called
detectEr
for runtime verifying asynchronous component systems
[
12
]
.
Whenever instrumentation aggregates a new trace to the history, the monitor is terminated and
the history analysis in
Figure

3
is invoked; this can be automated following an approach similar to that in
[
76
]
.
Trace aggregation and history analysis are
repeated until a permanent verdict is reached (
Proposition

IV.3
).

Complexity Bounds.

The algorithm’s performance depends on:

1.

The trace aggregation of
Figure

2
.
Monitors analyse system events sequentially and transition accordingly, each monitor component incurring a linear complexity w.r.t. the length of the processed trace.
The required number of monitor components and the cost of simulating these with a single monitor component has been studied extensively for similar monitoring systems in
[
68
]
.
There, the authors prove that monitors without parallel components may require up to a doubly-exponential number of states w.r.t. the size of the formula that they monitor.
This means that it may be necessary to maintain an exponentially long description of the monitor configurations along a run.
Under the assumption that formulae are generally significantly smaller than execution traces, or that monitors run asynchronously w.r.t. the SUS, the resulting overhead is acceptable.

2.

The history analysis of
Figure

3
.
The complexity of derivations for
\textbf{rej}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{Det}}(H,m)
is polynomial w.r.t. the size of
m
and the longest trace in
H
.
Effectively, this amounts to
\mu
-calculus model-checking on trees,
i.e.,
to modal logic model-checking on acyclic graphs, which requires a bilinear time w.r.t. the size of the tree and the formula
[
77
]
.
With the exception of rules
parAL
,
parAR
,
act
and
actPre
, derivations are mostly syntax-directed and monitors are guarded,
i.e.,
rule
rec
can only be applied a finite number of times before rule
act
is used.
For a similar (but more complex) tableau format,
[
76
, Section 5]
showed that, in practice, the doubly-exponential worst-case complexity upper bound identified in
[
24
]
does not represent the average-case complexity.

3.

The number of traces required by the monitor conducting the verification to reject the aggregated history.

Theorem

VII.4
contributes towards this, but formally answering it is hard since for certain formulae, an upper bound does not exist.
We revisit this aspect in
Example

VII.3
.

Appendix G
Actor Systems Formalised

We validate the realisation of ILTSs from
Section

II
and how realistic the constraints adopted in
Section

V
are by considering an instantiation for Actor-based systems
[
45
,
46
]
.
This concurrency model has been adopted by numerous programming languages
[
47
,
48
,
49
,
50
]
.
Actor systems are characterised by a set of processes called
actors
that interact with one another via
asynchronous message-passing
.
Every actor is identified by a unique ID, which is used by other actors to send messages to it
i.e.,
the
single-receiver
property.
Actors are
persistently receptive
meaning that they are always able to receive messages addressed to them.

Erlang Syntax for Actor Systems

\displaystyle A,B\in\textsc{Actr}

\displaystyle\mathrel{::=}\ \mathbf{0}\;\mid\;i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\;\mid\;i\langle v\rangle\;\mid\;A\parallel B\;\mid\;(\nu\,i)A\qquad\quad q,r\in\textsc{MBox}\mathrel{::=}\ \epsilon\;\mid\;v\mathop{:}q\qquad\quad p,o\in\textsc{Pat}\mathrel{::=}\ \textsl{x}\;\mid\;i\;\mid\;a

\displaystyle e,d\in\textsc{Exp}

\displaystyle\mathrel{::=}\ w_{1}\textsf{!}w_{2}.e\;\mid\;\mathsf{rcv}\,\{p_{n}\rightarrow e_{n}\}_{n\in I}\;\mid\;\mathsf{spw}\,d\,\mathsf{as}\,\textsl{x}.e\;\mid\;\mathsf{self}\,\textsl{x}.e\;\mid\;\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.e\;\mid\;\mathsf{X}\;\mid\;\mathbf{0}

Erlang Semantics for Actor Systems

\frac{}{K\;|\;O\triangleright i\mathsf{[}j\textsf{!}v.e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\parallel j\langle v\rangle}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}A^{\prime}\qquad\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\eta)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(B)}{K\;|\;O\triangleright A\parallel B\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}A^{\prime}\parallel B}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize parL}}\quad\frac{j\,\sharp\,K}{K\;|\;O\triangleright j\mathsf{[}d\,\triangleleft\,\epsilon\mathsf{]})}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize spw}}\quad\frac{}{K\;|\;O\triangleright sSwp}}\frac{i\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A)}{A\parallel(\nu\,i)B\equiv(\nu\,i)(A\parallel B)}\quad\textsc{{\scriptsize sExt}}

Fig. 6:
Language for Actor Systems

Figure

6
presents the syntax of our model actor language.
This grammar
assumes a set of
disjoint
actor names/addresses
i,j,h,k\in\textsc{Pid}
, atoms
a,b\in\textsc{Atom}
, expression variables
\textsl{x},\textsl{y}\in\textsc{Vars}
, and term variables
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\textsc{TVar}
.
Values,
v\in\textsc{Val}
, range over
\textsc{Pid}\cup\textsc{Atom}
and can be sent as messages.
Identifiers,
w
, are syntactic entities that range over values and variables.
An actor system,
A,B\in\textsc{Actr}
, consists of multiple parallel actors
A\parallel B
, which can either be inactive,
\mathbf{0}
, or locally
scoped
to a subsystem of actors,

(\nu\,i)A
.
A system may also have a number messages in transit; a message in the ether carrying value
v
addressed to
i
is denoted as
i\langle v\rangle
.
Individual actors,
i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}
, are uniquely identifiable by their name,
i
,
and consist of a running expression
e
and a mailbox
q
,
i.e.,
a list of values denoting a message queue.
Incoming messages are added at the end of the queue, whereas pattern-matched messages are removed from the front of the queue.
We use
q\mathop{:}r
to denote queue concatenation,
v\mathop{:}q
for the mailbox with
v
and
q
at the head and tail of the queue, and
q\mathop{:}v
for the mailbox with
v
at the end of the queue.
When the mailbox is empty,
\epsilon
, we often elide it from the individual actor and write
i\mathsf{[}e\mathsf{]}
instead of
i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,\epsilon\mathsf{]}
.
Actor expressions
e,d\in\textsc{Exp}
can be outputs,
w_{1}\textsf{!}w_{2}.e
, or reading inputs from the mailbox through pattern-matching,
\mathsf{rcv}\,\{p_{n}\rightarrow e_{n}\}_{n\in I}
, where each expression
e_{n}
is guarded by pattern
p_{n}
.
We assume patterns are disjoint,
i.e.,
if some
v
matches
p_{i}
, it does not match any other
p_{j}
for
i\neq j
and
i,j\in K
.
Expressions can also consist of self references (to the actor’s own name),

\mathsf{self}\,\textsl{x}.e
,
actor spawning,
\mathsf{spw}\,d\,\mathsf{as}\,\textsl{x}.e
,
or recursion,
\mathsf{rec}\,\mathsf{X}.e
.

We assume the standard definitions
\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A)
and

\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(A)

for the free names/variables of an actor system
A
and work up to
\alpha
-conversion of bound names/variables.
We also write
\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A)
for the free names
i
of the individual actors
i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}
in
A
.

E.g.
for

A=i\mathsf{[}e_{1}\mathsf{]}\parallel j\mathsf{[}e_{2}\mathsf{]}\parallel(\nu\,h)h\mathsf{[}e_{3}\mathsf{]}
,
we have
\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A)=\{i,j\}
and
\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A)=\{i,j\}\cup\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(e_{1})\cup\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(e_{2})\cup(\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(e_{1})\setminus\left\{h\right\})
.
Running actor systems are closed,
i.e.,

\mathop{\textsl{fv}}(A){=}\emptyset
and respect the single receiver property,

i.e.,
if
A=B_{1}\parallel B_{2}
then
\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(B_{1})\cap\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(B_{2})=\emptyset
.
For syntactic objects
o,o^{\prime}
, we write
o\,\sharp\,o^{\prime}
to mean that the free names of
o
and
o^{\prime}
are disjoint,
e.g.

A\,\sharp\,B
denotes
\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A)\cap\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(B){=}\emptyset
.
We also write
K,d

to mean
K{\uplus}\{d\}
,
where
\uplus
denotes disjoint union,
Substitutions are partial maps from variables to values,
\sigma{\in}\textsc{Sub}\!:\!\textsc{Vars}\rightharpoonup\textsc{Val}
.

The operational semantics of our language
is given in terms of an ILTS.

Knowledge

K\subseteq\textsc{Pid}

denotes the set of names known by an actor system
A
and an implicit observer with which it interacts;
K
is used by the rules in
Figure

6
to keep track of bound/free names and abstract away from name bindings in actions
[
51
,
35
]
; see
[
52
]
.
The
observer

O\subseteq\textsc{Pid}
is represented by the set of addresses
that
A
interacts with.
Transitions are defined over system states of the form
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\in\textsc{Prc}
where

\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A)\subseteq K
,
O\subseteq K
and
\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A)\,\sharp\,O
(respecting the single receiver property).
The ILTS transitions of the form

K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright B

are governed by the judgement

K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}B

defined by the rules in
Figure

6
.
The evolution of
K
and
O
after
\eta
is left implicit in

K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}B

since it is determined by the function
\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,\eta)
(below).

Definition G.1
.

\textsl{aft}(K\,|\,O,\eta)
is inductively defined as follows:

\displaystyle\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,i\textsf{!}v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}K\;|\;O\quad\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,i{\uparrow}v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}K\cup\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(v)\;|\;O

\displaystyle\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}K\;|\;O\quad\textsl{aft}(K\,|\,O,i?v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}K{\cup}\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(v)\,|\,O{\cup}(\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(v){\setminus}K)

\displaystyle\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,\mathsf{ncom})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}K\;|\;O\!\!\quad\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,\mathsf{com}(i,v))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{\tiny def}}}{{=}}K\;|\;O

\hfill\blacksquare

Actors communicate through
asynchronous messages, which are sent in two stages: the actor first creates a message
j\langle v\rangle
in the ether
(rule
snd1
), and then the ether sends value
v
to actor
j
(rule
snd2
).
Once received, messages are appended to the recipient’s local mailbox (rule
rcv
)
and
selectively
read following rule
rd
.
This relies on the
helper functions
\mathop{\textsl{absent}}(-)
and
\mathop{\textsl{match}}(-)
in
Definition

H.1
to find the first message
v
in the mailbox that matches one of the
patterns
p_{m}
in
\{p_{n}\!\xrightarrow{\;}\!e_{n}\}_{n\in I}
.
If a match is found, the actor branches to
e_{m}\sigma
, where
e_{m}
is the expression guarded by the matching pattern
p_{m}
and
\sigma
substitutes the free variables in
e_{m}
for the values resulting from the pattern-match.
Otherwise, reading blocks.
Parallel actors,
A\!\parallel\!B
, may
internally communicate
via rule
ncommL
whenever
A
and
B
can respectively transition with
dual output and input actions,
i\textsf{!}v
and
i?v
, binding all names extruded by
v

in the process,
or via rule
commL

if all names in
v
are already known
(symmetric rules
ncommR
,
commR
elided).
Actors may also transition independently
with rule
parL
(symmetric rule
parR
elided);
the
condition
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\mu)\!\,\sharp\,\!\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(B)
enforces the
single-receiver property
and checks
the message is not destined for
an actor in
B
.
An actor may also
scope extrude
names by communicating
bound names to actors outside the scope (rule
opn
).
Dually, when bound
names are not mentioned in the action along which the transition occurs or the action denotes internal communication
\mathsf{ncom}
, the names remain bound (rules
scp1
,
scp2
).
The remaining rules,
slf
and
spw
, are standard.
Our ILTS semantics uses structural equivalence for actor systems
A{\equiv}B
,
lifted as the process equivalence relation from
Section

II
,
i.e.,

(K_{1}\,|\,O_{1}\triangleright A_{1})\equiv(K_{2}\;|\;O_{2}\triangleright A_{2})
whenever
K_{1}=K_{2}
,
O_{1}=O_{2}
and
A_{1}\equiv A_{2}
.

G-A

Actor Structural Equivalence and Silent Actions

To show that our semantics is indeed an ILTS, we need to prove a few additional properties.

Proposition

VI.1
below shows that transitions abstract over structurally-equivalent states.

See
VI.1

As a result of
Proposition

VI.2
below, we are guaranteed that any actor SUS instrumented via a mechanism that implements the semantics in
Figure

2
can safely abstract over (non-traceable) silent transitions because they are confluent w.r.t. other actions.

See
VI.2

G-B

Actor Traceable Actions

Our actor semantics uses three forms of external actions,

eAct

\displaystyle=\{\,i?v,\ i\textsf{!}v,\ i{\uparrow}j\,\mid\,i,j\in\textsc{Pid},v\in\textsc{Val}\,\}

Apart from input actions,
i?v
, and output actions,
i\textsf{!}v
, we identify a specific form of outputs,
i{\uparrow}j
, where the payload
j
is
scope-extruded
to the observer, which manifests itself as
j\notin K
in our setting.
See rule
opn
in
Figure

6
.
Our semantics also employs two forms of internal actions,

\displaystyle\textsc{iAct}=\{\,\mathsf{com}(i,v),\ \mathsf{ncom}\,\mid\,i\in\textsc{Pid},v\in\textsc{Val}\,\}

We model actor communication via
internal communication actions
,
\mathsf{com}(i,v)
, as opposed to using silent actions as is standard in
[
35
,
51
]
.
This permits the instrumented monitors to differentiate between different communication steps which can reach states that are not necessarily behaviourally equivalent.
The exception to this strategy
is internal communication involving scoped names,
\mathsf{ncom}
; see rules
ncommL
and
scp2
in in
Figure

6
.
We still allow our monitor instrumentation to differentiate these transitions from silent actions, mainly because they do not satisfy properties such as
Proposition

VI.2
, and thus treat them differently during runtime verification.

Our ILTS interpretation treats input, output and internal communication actions as deterministic;
This treatment is justified by

Propositions

G.1
,
G.2
and
G.3
.
For the full proofs, refer to the dedicated sections,
Sections

H-E
,
H-F
and
H-G
.

Proposition G.1

(Input Determinacy)
.

If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime\prime}
then
A^{\prime}\equiv A^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on the two transitions, relying also on the single-receiver property.
∎

Proposition G.2

(Output Determinacy)
.

If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}A^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}A^{\prime\prime}
then
A^{\prime}\equiv A^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on the two transitions, relying also on
\equiv
from
Figure

6
.
∎

Proposition G.3

(Communication Determinacy)
.

If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{com}(i,v)\;}A^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{com}(i,v)\;}A^{\prime\prime}
then
A^{\prime}\!\equiv\!A^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on the two transitions, relying also on
Propositions

G.1
and
G.2
.
∎

Appendix H
Properties of Actor Systems

We formalise (resp. prove) the omitted definitions (resp. results) from
Section

VI
.
In particular, the
subject
of an action
is defined as
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\tau)=\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\mathsf{com}(i,v))=\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\mathsf{ncom})=\emptyset

and
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(c?d)=\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(c\textsf{!}d)=\{c\}
.

Definition

H.1
below describes
the two helper functions
\mathop{\textsl{absent}}(-)
and
\mathop{\textsl{match}}(-)
in
Figure

6
.

Definition H.1

(Pattern matching)
.

We define
\textsl{match}:\textsc{Pat}\times\textsc{Val}\xrightarrow{\;\;\;}\textsc{Sub}\cup\{\bot\}
and
\textsl{absent}:\textsc{Pat}\times\textsc{MBox}\xrightarrow{\;\;\;}\textsc{Bool}
as

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{match}}(p,v)

\displaystyle{=}\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{if }p=v=i\text{ or }p=v=i=a\\
\{\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$v$}\!/\!\mbox{\small$\textsl{x}$}\}&\text{if }p=\textsl{x}\\
\biguplus_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}&\text{if }p=\{p_{1},...,p_{n}\},\;v{=}\{v_{1},...,v_{n}\},\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\forall i{\in}\{1,...,n\}\cdot\mathop{\textsl{match}}(p_{i},v_{i}){=}\sigma_{i}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2}&\text{if }\textbf{dom}(\sigma_{1})\cap\textbf{dom}(\sigma_{2})=\emptyset\\
\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2}&\text{if }\forall v\in\textbf{dom}(\sigma_{1})\cap\textbf{dom}(\sigma_{2}),\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\sigma_{1}(v)=\sigma_{2}(v)\\
\bot&\text{if }\sigma_{1}=\bot\text{ or }\sigma_{2}=\bot\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{absent}}(p,\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{true}

\displaystyle\mathop{\textsl{absent}}(p,v\mathop{:}q)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathsf{false}&\text{if }\mathop{\textsl{match}}(p,v)=\bot\\
\mathop{\textsl{absent}}(p,q)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

∎

H-A

General Results

We give some general properties of actor systems that will be used in the following sections.

Lemma H.1
.

If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
then
i\in\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A)
.

Proof.

Straightforward by rule induction.
∎

Corollary 5
.

If
i\notin\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A)
then
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.47247pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 11.47247pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.8906pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.8906pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}}}}B
.

Proof.

Straightforward by rule induction.
∎

Lemma H.2
.

If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}B
then
\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(B)\subseteq\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A)
.

Proof.

Straightforward by rule induction.
∎

Lemma

H.3
below states that if a system can perform an input action, then that transition is always possible, regardless of the external observer
O
w.r.t. which it is executing.

Lemma H.3
.

If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
then
K\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
for every observer
O^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
.

•

Case
rcv
,
i.e.,

K\;|\;O\triangleright i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}
.
Our result,
K\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}
, follows
by rule
rcv
.

•

Case
scp1
,
i.e.,

K\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,j)A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}(\nu\,j)B

because
K,j\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
and
j\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(i?v)
.
By the IH, we obtain
K,j\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
.
Our result,
K\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright(\nu\,j)A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}(\nu\,j)B
, follows by rule
scp1
.

•

Case
parL
,
i.e.,

K\;|\;O\triangleright A\parallel B\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime}\parallel B

because
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime}
and
i\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(B)
.
By the IH, we obtain
K\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime}
.
Our result,
K\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A\parallel B\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime}\parallel B
, follows by
parL
.

•

Case
parR
. The proof is analogous to that for
parL
. ∎

Similarly,
Lemma

H.4

states that if a system can
\tau
-transition, then that transition is always possible, regardless of the knowledge
K
and external observer
O
w.r.t. which it is executing.

Lemma H.4
.

K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K\;|\;O\triangleright B
implies
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright B
for all knowledge
K,K^{\prime}
and observers
O,O^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by rule induction.
∎

H-B

Inversion Lemmas

We also prove several results that provide insights into the structure and behaviour of actor systems.

Lemma H.5
.

If
A\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
then one of the following statements must hold:

•

A=A_{1}
and
A_{2}=\mathbf{0}
, or
A=A_{2}
and
A_{1}=\mathbf{0}

•

A_{1}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{1})A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{2})A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
A_{2}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{3})A_{2}^{\prime}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{4})A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and

A=(\nu\,\vec{h}_{1},\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{3},\vec{h}_{4})(B_{1}\parallel B_{2})
and

B_{1}=A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
and

B_{2}=A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on
A\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
.
∎

Lemma H.6
.

If
A\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
then

1.

either

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A_{1}^{\prime}
and
B\equiv A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
;

2.

or

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A_{2}^{\prime}
and
B\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
A\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
.
The proof proceeds by rule induction on the latter.

•

Case
rcv
,
i.e.,

A=i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}
and
A^{\prime}=i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}
.
Since
A=A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
, then by
Lemma

H.5
, we must have either
A=A_{1}
and
A_{2}=\mathbf{0}
, or
A=A_{2}
and
A_{1}=\mathbf{0}
.
In the first case, condition (1) is satisfied since
i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}\equiv i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}\parallel\mathbf{0}
.
Otherwise, condition (2) is satisfied since
i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}\equiv\mathbf{0}\parallel i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}
.

•

Case
parL
,
i.e.,

A=A_{3}\parallel A_{4}
and
B=B_{3}\parallel A_{4}
because
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{3}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{3}
.
By
Lemma

H.5
and
A=A_{3}\parallel A_{4}
, we must have

A_{1}\equiv A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
A_{2}\equiv A_{2}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and

A_{3}=A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
and
A_{4}=A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.
Using the facts that
A_{3}=A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{3}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{3}
and the IH, we know that

\displaystyle\text{either }K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }B_{3}\equiv B_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{or }K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{2}^{\prime}\text{ and }B_{3}\equiv A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel B_{2}^{\prime}

Applying rule
parL
on the transitions and rule
sCtxP
on the equivalences, these respectively give us that either

\displaystyle K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }B_{3}\parallel A_{4}\equiv(B_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime})\parallel A_{4}

or

\displaystyle K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{2}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }B_{3}\parallel A_{4}\equiv(A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel B_{2}^{\prime})\parallel A_{4}

Using
A_{4}=A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
,
A_{1}=A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
A_{2}=A_{2}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
,
B=B_{3}\parallel A_{4}
and rules for
\equiv
, we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\text{either }K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }B\equiv(B_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{1}^{\prime\prime})\parallel A_{2}

\displaystyle\text{or }K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{2}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }B\equiv A_{1}\parallel(B_{2}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime})

which correspond to conditions (1) and (2).

•

Case
parR
, similar to that for
parL
.

•

Case
scp1
,
i.e.,

A=(\nu\,j)A^{\prime}
and
B=(\nu\,j)A^{\prime\prime}
because

K,j\;|\;O\triangleright A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime\prime}
and
j\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(i,v)
.
By
Lemma

H.5
, there are two subcases to consider:

–

When
A=A_{1}
and
A_{2}=\mathbf{0}
, by this and
K\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,j)A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}(\nu\,j)A^{\prime\prime}
, we know
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}(\nu\,j)A^{\prime\prime}
.
By rule
sNil
, we conclude
B=(\nu\,j)A^{\prime\prime}\equiv B\parallel\mathbf{0}=B\parallel A_{2}
as required.

–

When
A_{1}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{1})A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{2})A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and

A_{2}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{3})A_{2}^{\prime}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{4})A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
such that

\displaystyle A=(\nu\,\vec{h}_{1},\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{3},\vec{h}_{4})(A_{3}\parallel A_{4})\text{ where }

\displaystyle A_{3}=A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad A_{4}=A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}

Since
A=(\nu\,j)A^{\prime}
, we must have that
j\in\vec{h}_{i}
for some
i\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
.
Consider the case for when
i=1
; other cases follow with similar reasoning.
Let
\vec{h}_{5}=\vec{h}_{1}\setminus\{j\}
.
Then we know

\displaystyle A^{\prime}=(\nu\,\vec{h}_{5},\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{3},\vec{h}_{4})(A_{3}\parallel A_{4})

(8)

Since we work up to
\alpha
-conversion of bound entities, we can assume that

\vec{h}_{1}\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A_{4})
and
\vec{h}_{3}\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A_{4})

and
\vec{h}_{2}\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A_{3})
and
\vec{h}_{4}\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(A_{3})
.
Using the fact that
\vec{h}_{5}\subset\vec{h}_{1}
and the rules defining
\equiv
in
Figure

6
, we also know

\displaystyle A^{\prime}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{5},\vec{h}_{3})A_{3}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{4})A_{4}

(9)

By (
8
), the fact that
K,j\;|\;O\triangleright A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}A^{\prime\prime}
and the IH, we obtain that either

\displaystyle K,j\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,\vec{h}_{5},\vec{h}_{3})A_{3}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{1}\text{ and }A^{\prime\prime}\equiv B_{1}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{4})A_{4}

or

(10)

\displaystyle K,j\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{4})A_{4}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{2}\text{ and }A^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{5},\vec{h}_{3})A_{3}\parallel B_{2}

(11)

If (
10
) holds, applying rules
scp1
,
sCtxS
,
sExt
,
sCom
gives us that

\displaystyle(\nu\,\vec{h}_{1},\vec{h}_{3})A_{3}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}(\nu\,j)B_{1}\text{ and }

\displaystyle(\nu\,j)A^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\nu\,j)B_{1}\parallel(\nu\,\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{4})A_{4}

which corresponds to statement
(1)
as required.
If (
11
) holds, applying rules
scp1
,
sCtxS
,
sExt
,
sCom
give us

\displaystyle K\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,j,\vec{h}_{2},\vec{h}_{4})A_{4}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}(\nu\,j)B_{2}\text{ and }

\displaystyle(\nu\,j)A^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\nu\,\vec{h}_{5},\vec{h}_{3})A_{3}\parallel(\nu\,j)B_{2}

which corresponds to statement
(2)
as required.

•

Case
sTrn
, proof is straightforward. ∎

Corollary 6
.

If
i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\equiv A
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B
then
B\equiv i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}
.

Proof.

Follows from
Lemma

H.6
since

K\;|\;O\triangleright i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathop{:}v\mathsf{]}
and

i\mathsf{[}e\,\triangleleft\,q\mathsf{]}\equiv A\parallel\mathbf{0}
.
∎

Lemma H.7
.

If
A\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}B
then

1.

either
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}A_{1}^{\prime}
and
B\equiv A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
;

2.

or
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}A_{2}^{\prime}
and
B\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that for
Lemma

H.6
.
∎

Lemma H.8
.

If
A\equiv(\nu\,i)A^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}B
and
i\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fn}}(\eta)
then

B\equiv(\nu\,i)B^{\prime}
and
K,i\;|\;O\triangleright A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}B^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Proof is straightforward.
∎

Lemma H.9
.

If
A\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}B
then one of the following statements must hold:

1.

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}A_{1}^{\prime}
and
B\equiv A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
and
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\eta)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})

2.

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}A_{2}^{\prime}
and
B\equiv A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\eta)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})

3.

\eta=\mathsf{com}(i,v)

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}B_{1}

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{2}

and
B\equiv B_{1}\parallel B_{2}

4.

\eta=\mathsf{com}(i,v)

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i?v\;}B_{1}

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i\textsf{!}v\;}B_{2}

and
B\equiv B_{1}\parallel B_{2}

5.

\eta=\mathsf{ncom}

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i{\uparrow}j\;}B_{1}

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i?j\;}B_{2}

and
B\equiv(\nu\,j)(B_{1}\parallel B_{2})

6.

\eta=\mathsf{ncom}

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i?j\;}B_{1}

and
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i{\uparrow}j\;}B_{2}

and
B\equiv(\nu\,j)(B_{1}\parallel B_{2})

Proof.

We omit the proof due to its length.
However, it can be proven via rule induction on
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}B
, using also
Lemma

H.5
.
The method is similar to that for
Lemma

H.6
.
∎

H-C

Actor Structural Equivalence and Silent Actions

We prove
Proposition

VI.1
from
Section

VI
.
This result states that transitions abstract over structurally-equivalent states.

See
VI.1

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}A^{\prime}
.
∎

We can also show that (non-traceable) silent transitions are confluent w.r.t. other actions,
Proposition

VI.2
.

See
VI.2

Proof.

Intuitively, this is true because if
K\;|\;O\triangleright A
does two
different
moves
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}A^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}A^{\prime\prime}
, then both moves must have occurred in different components of
A
.
The proof proceeds by induction on the derivation of the first move,
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}A^{\prime}
.

All axioms are trivial.
Rules
commL
,
commR
,
ncommL
,
ncommR
,
scp2
and
opn
do not occur in any derivation of a
\tau
move.
For the inductive cases, the only non-straightforward rule is
parL
(the proof for
parR
is analogous).
If
K\;|\;O\triangleright A\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K\;|\;O\triangleright A^{\prime}
was derived using this rule, then

\displaystyle K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}\quad

\displaystyle\text{ because }K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}

(12)

We examine the proof for the second move, which must be of the form
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}\textsl{aft}(K|O,\eta)\triangleright A^{\prime\prime}
.
By
Lemma

H.9
, there are six possible ways how this could have occurred.
We focus on the main cases:

•

A^{\prime\prime}=A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}K^{\prime}\triangleright A_{2}^{\prime}
where
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}=\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,\eta)
and
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\eta)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})
.
Diagrammatically

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
\tau
\eta

From
Lemma

H.4
and (
12
), we get

K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}
.

Since
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\tau)=\emptyset
, thus
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\tau)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})
, we can apply rule
parL
to get the move

K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
.

By
Lemma

H.2
, we also know
\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})
, which implies
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\eta)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1}^{\prime})
.
Rule
parR
can thus be applied to
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{2}^{\prime}
to obtain the move
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
.
This gives us the required commuting diagram

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime}
\tau
\eta
\eta
\tau

•

A^{\prime\prime}=A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
and
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}
where
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}=\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;O,\eta)
and
\mathop{\textsl{sbj}}(\eta)\,\sharp\,\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})
.
In other words, we have to complete the diagram

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}
\tau
\eta
\eta
\tau

But note that we also have the diagram

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
\tau
\eta

that can be completed by induction as

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
\tau
\eta
\eta
\tau

Applying
parL
twice on

K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\tau\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\eta\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
give the two required moves.

•

\eta=\mathsf{ncom}
and
A^{\prime\prime}=(\nu\,j)\bigl(A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)

and

K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}\xrightarrow{\;i{\uparrow}j\;}K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and

K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1})\triangleright A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;i?j\;}K^{\prime\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime\prime}\triangleright A_{2}^{\prime\prime}

for some name
i,j\in\textsc{Pid}

K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}=\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2}),i{\uparrow}j)

and
K^{\prime\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime\prime}=\textsl{aft}(K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{1}),i?j)
.
In other words, we have to complete the diagram

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}\parallel A_{2}
K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}
K\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,j)\bigl(A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)
K\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,j)\bigl(A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)
\tau
\mathsf{ncom}
\mathsf{ncom}
\tau

But note that we also have the diagram

K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}
K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}
K^{\prime}\;|\;O^{\prime}\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
\tau

K\;|\;\mathop{\textsl{fId}}(A_{2})\triangleright A_{1}

K\;|\;O\triangleright A_{1}^{\prime}\parallel A_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{ncom}\;}K\;|\;O\triangleright(\nu\,j)(A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\parallel A_{2}^{\prime\prime})

and

The remaining cases follow with similar reasoning.
